<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Northern_Ireland" title="Languages of Northern Ireland">Languages of Northern Ireland</a></div><p>Irish is an official language of Northern Ireland as of 6 December 2022 when the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Language_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Language Act">Irish Language Act</a> (Identity and Language (Northern Ireland) Act 2022) became law. The Irish Language Act officially repealed legislation from 1737 that banned the use of Irish in courts.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> English is a <i>de facto</i> official language.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> English is also spoken as a first language by 95.4% of the Northern Ireland population.<sup id="cite_ref-2022_Language_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2022_Language-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> </p><div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png/220px-English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png/330px-English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png/440px-English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="833" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:English_dialects_in_Ulster_contrast.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Approximate boundaries of the current and historical English/<a href="/wiki/Scots_language" title="Scots language">Scots</a> dialects in <a href="/wiki/Ulster" title="Ulster">Ulster</a>. South to north, the colour bands represent <a href="/wiki/Hiberno-English" title="Hiberno-English">Hiberno-English</a>, South-<a href="/wiki/Ulster_English" title="Ulster English">Ulster English</a>, Mid-Ulster English, and the three traditional <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Scots_dialects" class="mw-redirect" title="Ulster Scots dialects">Ulster Scots</a> areas. The Irish-speaking <a href="/wiki/Gaeltacht" title="Gaeltacht">Gaeltacht</a> is not shown.</div></div></div>
<p>Under the <a href="/wiki/Belfast_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Belfast Agreement">Good Friday Agreement</a>, Irish and <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Scots_dialects" class="mw-redirect" title="Ulster Scots dialects">Ulster Scots</a> (an Ulster dialect of the <a href="/wiki/Scots_language" title="Scots language">Scots language</a>, sometimes known as <i>Ullans</i>), are recognised as "part of the cultural wealth of Northern Ireland".<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> The Irish Language Act of 2022 also legislated commissioners for both Irish and Ulster Scots.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two all-island bodies for the promotion of these were created under the Agreement: <i><a href="/wiki/Foras_na_Gaeilge" title="Foras na Gaeilge">Foras na Gaeilge</a></i>, which promotes the Irish language, and the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Scots_Agency" class="mw-redirect" title="Ulster Scots Agency">Ulster Scots Agency</a>, which promotes the Ulster-Scots dialect and culture. These operate separately under the aegis of <a href="/wiki/The_North/South_Language_Body" title="The North/South Language Body">the North/South Language Body</a>, which reports to the <a href="/wiki/North/South_Ministerial_Council" title="North/South Ministerial Council">North/South Ministerial Council</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/British_government" class="mw-redirect" title="British government">UK Government</a> in 2001 ratified the <a href="/wiki/European_Charter_for_Regional_or_Minority_Languages" title="European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages">European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages</a>. Irish (in Northern Ireland) was specified under Part III of the Charter, with a range of specific undertakings about education, translation of statutes, interaction with public authorities, the use of placenames, media access, support for cultural activities, and other matters. A lower level of recognition was accorded to Ulster-Scots, under Part II of the Charter.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="English">English</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Ulster_English" title="Ulster English">Ulster English</a></div>
<p>According to the 2021 Census, in 94.74% of households, all people aged 16 and above spoke English as their main language.<sup id="cite_ref-Census_2021_Phase_2_Bulletin_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census_2021_Phase_2_Bulletin-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> The dialect of English spoken in Northern Ireland shows influence from the lowland <a href="/wiki/Scots_language" title="Scots language">Scots language</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> There are supposedly some minute differences in pronunciation between Protestants and Catholics, for instance; the name of the letter <i>h</i>, which Protestants tend to pronounce as "aitch", as in <a href="/wiki/British_English" title="British English">British English</a>, and Catholics tend to pronounce as "haitch", as in <a href="/wiki/Hiberno-English" title="Hiberno-English">Hiberno-English</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> However, geography is a much more important determinant of dialect than religious background.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Irish">Irish</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png/220px-Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="171" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png/330px-Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png/440px-Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png 2x" data-file-width="18870" data-file-height="14693" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_those_with_some_ability_in_Irish_in_the_2011_census_in_Northern_Ireland.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Percentage of people aged 3+ claiming to have some ability in Irish in the 2011 census</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Irish_language_in_Northern_Ireland" title="Irish language in Northern Ireland">Irish language in Northern Ireland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Irish" title="Ulster Irish">Ulster Irish</a></div>
<p>The Irish language (<a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">an Ghaeilge</i>), or <i>Gaelic</i>, is the second most spoken language in Northern Ireland and is a native language of Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> It was spoken predominantly throughout what is now Northern Ireland before the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Plantations" class="mw-redirect" title="Ulster Plantations">Ulster Plantations</a> in the 17th century and most place names in Northern Ireland are <a href="/wiki/Anglicised" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglicised">anglicised</a> versions of a Gaelic name. Today, the language is often associated with Irish nationalism (and thus with Catholics). However, in the 19th century, the language was seen as a common heritage, with Ulster Protestants playing a leading role in the <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_revival" title="Gaelic revival">Gaelic revival</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 2021 census, 12.4% (compared with 10.7% in 2011) of the population of Northern Ireland claimed "some knowledge of Irish" and 3.9% (compared with 3.7% in 2011) reported being able to "speak, read, write and understand" Irish.<sup id="cite_ref-Census_2011_148-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census_2011-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2022_Language_171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2022_Language-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> In another survey, from 1999, 1% of respondents said they spoke it as their main language at home.<sup id="cite_ref-Survey-NorthernIreland_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Survey-NorthernIreland-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The dialect spoken in Northern Ireland, Ulster Irish, has two main types, East Ulster Irish and Donegal Irish (or West Ulster Irish),<sup id="cite_ref-pipex1_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pipex1-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> is the one closest to <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic" title="Scottish Gaelic">Scottish Gaelic</a> (which developed into a separate language from Irish Gaelic in the 17th century). Some words and phrases are shared with Scots Gaelic, and the dialects of east Ulster – those of <a href="/wiki/Rathlin_Island" title="Rathlin Island">Rathlin Island</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Glens_of_Antrim" title="Glens of Antrim">Glens of Antrim</a> – were very similar to the dialect of <a href="/wiki/Argyll" title="Argyll">Argyll</a>, the part of Scotland nearest to Ireland. And those dialects of Armagh and Down were also very similar to the dialects of Galloway.
</p><p>The use of the Irish language in Northern Ireland today is politically sensitive. The erection by some <a href="/wiki/Local_government_in_Northern_Ireland" title="Local government in Northern Ireland">district councils</a> of bilingual street names in both English and Irish,<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> invariably in predominantly nationalist districts, is resisted by unionists who claim that it creates a "chill factor" and thus harms community relationships. Efforts by members of the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Assembly" title="Northern Ireland Assembly">Northern Ireland Assembly</a> to legislate for some official uses of the language have failed to achieve the required cross-community support. In May 2022, the UK Government proposed a bill in the House of Lords to make Irish an official language (and support Ulster Scots) in Northern Ireland and to create an Irish Language Commissioner.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> There has recently been an increase in interest in the language among unionists in East Belfast.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ulster_Scots">Ulster Scots</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png/220px-Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="171" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png/330px-Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png/440px-Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png 2x" data-file-width="19184" data-file-height="14938" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_showing_the_percentage_of_people_aged_3%2B_claiming_to_have_some_ability_in_Ulster_Scots_in_the_2011_census.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Percentage of people aged 3+ claiming to have some ability in Ulster Scots in the 2011 census</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Scots_dialects" class="mw-redirect" title="Ulster Scots dialects">Ulster Scots dialects</a></div>
<p>Ulster Scots comprises varieties of the <a href="/wiki/Scots_language" title="Scots language">Scots language</a> spoken in Northern Ireland. For a native English speaker, "[Ulster Scots] is comparatively accessible, and even at its most intense can be understood fairly easily with the help of a glossary."<sup id="cite_ref-Poilin-BBC_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Poilin-BBC-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Along with the Irish language, the Good Friday Agreement recognised the dialect as part of Northern Ireland's unique culture and the <a href="/wiki/St_Andrews_Agreement" title="St Andrews Agreement">St Andrews Agreement</a> recognised the need to "enhance and develop the Ulster Scots language, heritage and culture".<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the time of the 2021 census, approximately 1.1% (compared to 0.9% in 2011) of the population claimed to be able to speak, read, write and understand Ulster-Scots, while 10.4% (compared to 8.1% in 2011) professed to have "some ability".<sup id="cite_ref-Census_2011_148-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Census_2011-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2022_Language_171-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2022_Language-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Survey-NorthernIreland_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Survey-NorthernIreland-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Sign_languages">Sign languages</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Sign_Language" title="Northern Ireland Sign Language">Northern Ireland Sign Language</a>, <a href="/wiki/Irish_Sign_Language" title="Irish Sign Language">Irish Sign Language</a>, and <a href="/wiki/British_Sign_Language" title="British Sign Language">British Sign Language</a></div>
<p>The most common <a href="/wiki/Sign_language" title="Sign language">sign language</a> in Northern Ireland is <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Sign_Language" title="Northern Ireland Sign Language">Northern Ireland Sign Language</a> (NISL). However, because in the past Catholic families tended to send their deaf children to schools in Dublin<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> where <a href="/wiki/Irish_Sign_Language" title="Irish Sign Language">Irish Sign Language</a> (ISL) is commonly used, ISL is still common among many older deaf people from Catholic families.
</p><p>Irish Sign Language (ISL) has some influence from the French family of sign language, which includes <a href="/wiki/American_Sign_Language" title="American Sign Language">American Sign Language</a> (ASL). NISL takes a large component from the British family of sign language (which also includes <a href="/wiki/Auslan" title="Auslan">Auslan</a>) with many borrowings from ASL. It is described as being related to Irish Sign Language at the <a href="/wiki/Syntactic" class="mw-redirect" title="Syntactic">syntactic</a> level while much of the <a href="/wiki/Lexicon" title="Lexicon">lexicon</a> is based on <a href="/wiki/British_Sign_Language" title="British Sign Language">British Sign Language</a> (BSL).<sup id="cite_ref-Janzen_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Janzen-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As of March&#160;2004<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Northern_Ireland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/British_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="British Government">UK Government</a> recognises only British Sign Language and Irish Sign Language as the official sign languages used in Northern Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-dcal_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dcal-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Northern_Ireland" title="Culture of Northern Ireland">Culture of Northern Ireland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade,_Belfast,_September_2012_(010).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade%2C_Belfast%2C_September_2012_%28010%29.JPG/220px-Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade%2C_Belfast%2C_September_2012_%28010%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade%2C_Belfast%2C_September_2012_%28010%29.JPG/330px-Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade%2C_Belfast%2C_September_2012_%28010%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade%2C_Belfast%2C_September_2012_%28010%29.JPG/440px-Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade%2C_Belfast%2C_September_2012_%28010%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ulster_Covenant_Commemoration_Parade,_Belfast,_September_2012_(010).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>An <a href="/wiki/Orange_Order" title="Orange Order">Orange</a> march</div></div></div>
<p>Northern Ireland shares both the <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Ireland" title="Culture of Ireland">culture of Ireland</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Culture of the United Kingdom">culture of the United Kingdom</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Parades_in_Northern_Ireland" title="Parades in Northern Ireland">Parades are a prominent feature</a> of Northern Ireland society,<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> more so than in the rest of Ireland or the United Kingdom. Most are held by Protestant fraternities such as the <a href="/wiki/Orange_Order" title="Orange Order">Orange Order</a>, and Ulster loyalist marching bands. Each summer, during the "marching season", these groups have hundreds of parades, <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_flags_issue" title="Northern Ireland flags issue">deck streets with British flags</a>, bunting and specially-made arches, and light large towering bonfires in the <a href="/wiki/Eleventh_Night" title="Eleventh Night">"Eleventh Night" celebrations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bryan130_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bryan130-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> The biggest parades are held on 12 July (<a href="/wiki/The_Twelfth" title="The Twelfth">The Twelfth</a>). There is often tension when these activities take place near Catholic neighbourhoods, which sometimes leads to violence.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since the end of the Troubles, Northern Ireland has witnessed rising numbers of tourists. Attractions include cultural festivals, musical and artistic traditions, countryside and geographical sites of interest, public houses, welcoming hospitality, and sports (especially golf and fishing). Since 1987 public houses have been allowed to open on Sundays, despite some opposition.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Cycle" title="Ulster Cycle">Ulster Cycle</a> is a large body of prose and verse centring on the traditional heroes of the <a href="/wiki/Ulaid" title="Ulaid">Ulaid</a> in what is now eastern Ulster. This is one of the four major cycles of <a href="/wiki/Irish_mythology" title="Irish mythology">Irish mythology</a>. The cycle centres on the reign of <a href="/wiki/Conchobar_mac_Nessa" title="Conchobar mac Nessa">Conchobar mac Nessa</a>, who is said to have been the king of Ulster around the 1st century. He ruled from <a href="/wiki/Emain_Macha" class="mw-redirect" title="Emain Macha">Emain Macha</a> (now Navan Fort near Armagh), and had a fierce rivalry with queen <a href="/wiki/Medb" title="Medb">Medb</a> and king Ailill of Connacht and their ally, <a href="/wiki/Fergus_mac_R%C3%B3ich" title="Fergus mac Róich">Fergus mac Róich</a>, former king of Ulster. The foremost hero of the cycle is Conchobar's nephew <a href="/wiki/C%C3%BAchulainn" class="mw-redirect" title="Cúchulainn">Cúchulainn</a>, who features in the <a href="/wiki/Epic_poetry" title="Epic poetry">epic prose/poem</a> <i>An <a href="/wiki/T%C3%A1in_B%C3%B3_C%C3%BAailnge" title="Táin Bó Cúailnge">Táin Bó Cúailnge</a></i> (The Cattle Raid of Cooley, a <i><a href="/wiki/Casus_belli" title="Casus belli">casus belli</a></i> between Ulster and <a href="/wiki/Connaught" class="mw-redirect" title="Connaught">Connaught</a>).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Symbols">Symbols</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_flags_issue" title="Northern Ireland flags issue">Northern Ireland flags issue</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:NI_Assembly.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/NI_Assembly.svg/220px-NI_Assembly.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/NI_Assembly.svg/330px-NI_Assembly.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/NI_Assembly.svg/440px-NI_Assembly.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="582" data-file-height="465" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:NI_Assembly.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The logo for the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Assembly" title="Northern Ireland Assembly">Northern Ireland assembly</a> is based on the flower of the <a href="/wiki/Flax" title="Flax">flax</a> plant.<sup id="cite_ref-NI_logo_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NI_logo-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_(1924%E2%80%931953).svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_%281924%E2%80%931953%29.svg/220px-Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_%281924%E2%80%931953%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_%281924%E2%80%931953%29.svg/330px-Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_%281924%E2%80%931953%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_%281924%E2%80%931953%29.svg/440px-Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_%281924%E2%80%931953%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="300" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_Northern_Ireland_(1924%E2%80%931953).svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Banner" title="Ulster Banner">Ulster Banner</a> is used to represent Northern Ireland in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_national_football_team" title="Northern Ireland national football team">association football</a> and at the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Games" title="Commonwealth Games">Commonwealth Games</a>.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Patricks_Day,_Belfast,_March_2015_(39).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/St_Patricks_Day%2C_Belfast%2C_March_2015_%2839%29.JPG/220px-St_Patricks_Day%2C_Belfast%2C_March_2015_%2839%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/St_Patricks_Day%2C_Belfast%2C_March_2015_%2839%29.JPG/330px-St_Patricks_Day%2C_Belfast%2C_March_2015_%2839%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/St_Patricks_Day%2C_Belfast%2C_March_2015_%2839%29.JPG/440px-St_Patricks_Day%2C_Belfast%2C_March_2015_%2839%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4896" data-file-height="3672" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Patricks_Day,_Belfast,_March_2015_(39).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>People carrying the <a href="/wiki/Irish_flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish flag">Irish flag</a>, overlooking those with the unionist <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Banner" title="Ulster Banner">Ulster Banner</a></div></div></div>
<p>Northern Ireland comprises a patchwork of communities whose national loyalties are represented in some areas by flags flown from flagpoles or lamp posts. The <a href="/wiki/Union_Jack" title="Union Jack">Union Jack</a> and the former <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Northern_Ireland" title="Flag of Northern Ireland">Northern Ireland flag</a> are flown in many loyalist areas, and the Tricolour, adopted by republicans as the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Ireland" title="Flag of Ireland">flag of Ireland</a> in 1916,<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> is flown in some republican areas. Even kerbstones in some areas are painted red-white-blue or green-white-orange, depending on whether local people express unionist/loyalist or nationalist/republican sympathies.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The official flag is that of the state having sovereignty over the territory, i.e. the Union Flag.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> The former Northern Ireland flag, also known as the "<a href="/wiki/Ulster_Banner" title="Ulster Banner">Ulster Banner</a>" or "Red Hand Flag", is a banner derived from the coat of arms of the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Northern_Ireland" title="Government of Northern Ireland">Government of Northern Ireland</a> until 1972. Since 1972, it has had no official status. The Union Flag and the Ulster Banner are used exclusively by unionists. The UK flags policy states that in Northern Ireland, "The Ulster flag and the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Saltire" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Saltire">Cross of St Patrick</a> have no official status and, under the Flags Regulations, are not permitted to be flown from Government Buildings."<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Irish_Rugby_Football_Union" title="Irish Rugby Football Union">Irish Rugby Football Union</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Ireland" title="Church of Ireland">Church of Ireland</a> have used the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Saltire" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Saltire">Saint Patrick's Saltire</a> or "Cross of St&#160;Patrick". This red <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">saltire</a> on a white field was used to represent Ireland in the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Flag of the United Kingdom">flag of the United Kingdom</a>. It is still used by some <a href="/wiki/British_army" class="mw-redirect" title="British army">British army</a> regiments. Foreign flags are also found, such as the <a href="/wiki/Palestinian_territories" title="Palestinian territories">Palestinian</a> flags in some nationalist areas and <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israeli</a> flags in some unionist areas.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The United Kingdom national anthem of "<a href="/wiki/God_Save_the_King" title="God Save the King">God Save the King</a>" is often played at state events in Northern Ireland. At the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Games" title="Commonwealth Games">Commonwealth Games</a> and some other sporting events, the Northern Ireland team uses the Ulster Banner as its flag—notwithstanding its lack of official status—and the <i><a href="/wiki/Londonderry_Air" title="Londonderry Air">Londonderry Air</a></i> (usually set to <a href="/wiki/Lyrics" title="Lyrics">lyrics</a> as <i><a href="/wiki/Danny_Boy" title="Danny Boy">Danny Boy</a></i>), which also has no official status, as its <a href="/wiki/National_anthem" title="National anthem">national anthem</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_national_football_team" title="Northern Ireland national football team">Northern Ireland national football team</a> also uses the Ulster Banner as its flag but uses "God Save The King" as its anthem.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
Major <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_Athletic_Association" title="Gaelic Athletic Association">Gaelic Athletic Association</a> matches are opened by the national anthem of the Republic of Ireland, "<a href="/wiki/Amhr%C3%A1n_na_bhFiann" title="Amhrán na bhFiann">Amhrán na bhFiann</a> (The Soldier's Song)", which is also used by most other all-Ireland sporting organisations.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
Since 1995, the <a href="/wiki/Ireland_rugby_union_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Ireland rugby union team">Ireland rugby union team</a> has used a specially commissioned song, "<a href="/wiki/Ireland%27s_Call" title="Ireland&#39;s Call">Ireland's Call</a>" as the team's anthem. The Irish national anthem is also played at Dublin home matches, being the anthem of the host country.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Northern_Irish_murals" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Irish murals">Northern Irish murals</a> have become well-known features of Northern Ireland, depicting past and present events and documenting peace and cultural diversity. Almost 2,000 murals have been documented in Northern Ireland since the 1970s.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sport">Sport</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sport_in_Northern_Ireland" title="Sport in Northern Ireland">Sport in Northern Ireland</a></div>
<p>In Northern Ireland, sport is popular and important in the lives of many people. Sports tend to be organised on an <a href="/wiki/All-Ireland" title="All-Ireland">all-Ireland</a> basis, with a single team for the whole island.<sup id="cite_ref-theherald1_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theherald1-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> The most notable exception is association football, which has separate governing bodies for each jurisdiction.<sup id="cite_ref-theherald1_205-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theherald1-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Field_sports">Field sports</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Association_football">Association football</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:George_best_1976.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/George_best_1976.jpg/170px-George_best_1976.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="235" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/George_best_1976.jpg/255px-George_best_1976.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/George_best_1976.jpg/340px-George_best_1976.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1112" data-file-height="1540" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:George_best_1976.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/George_Best" title="George Best">George Best</a>, <a href="/wiki/People_of_Northern_Ireland" title="People of Northern Ireland">Northern Irish</a> international footballer and 1968 <a href="/wiki/Ballon_d%27Or" title="Ballon d&#39;Or">Ballon d'Or</a></div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Irish_Football_Association" title="Irish Football Association">Irish Football Association</a> (IFA) serves as the organising body for association football in Northern Ireland, with the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Football_League" title="Northern Ireland Football League">Northern Ireland Football League</a> (NIFL) responsible for the independent administration of the three divisions of national domestic football, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Football_League_Cup" title="Northern Ireland Football League Cup">Northern Ireland Football League Cup</a>.
</p><p>The highest level of competition within Northern Ireland are the <a href="/wiki/NIFL_Premiership" title="NIFL Premiership">NIFL Premiership</a> and the <a href="/wiki/NIFL_Championship" title="NIFL Championship">NIFL Championship</a>. However, many players from Northern Ireland compete with clubs in <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a> and <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>.
</p><p>NIFL clubs are <a href="/wiki/Semi-professional" class="mw-redirect" title="Semi-professional">semi-professional</a> or Intermediate.<a href="/wiki/NIFL_Premiership" title="NIFL Premiership">NIFL Premiership</a> clubs are also eligible to compete in the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Champions_League" title="UEFA Champions League">UEFA Champions League</a> and <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Europa_League" title="UEFA Europa League">UEFA Europa League</a> with the league champions entering the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Champions_League" title="UEFA Champions League">Champions league</a> second qualifying round and the 2nd placed league finisher, the European play-off winners and the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Cup" title="Irish Cup">Irish Cup</a> winners entering the <a href="/wiki/Europa_League" class="mw-redirect" title="Europa League">Europa League</a> second qualifying round. No clubs have ever reached the group stage.
</p><p>Despite Northern Ireland's small population, the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_national_football_team" title="Northern Ireland national football team">Northern Ireland national football team</a> qualified for the <a href="/wiki/1958_FIFA_World_Cup" title="1958 FIFA World Cup">1958 FIFA World Cup</a>, <a href="/wiki/1982_FIFA_World_Cup" title="1982 FIFA World Cup">1982 FIFA World Cup</a> and <a href="/wiki/1986_FIFA_World_Cup" title="1986 FIFA World Cup">1986 FIFA World Cup</a>, making it to the quarter-finals in 1958 and 1982 and made it the first knockout round in the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_European_Championship" title="UEFA European Championship">European Championships</a> in 2016.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Rugby_union">Rugby union</span></h4>
<p>The six counties of Northern Ireland are among the nine governed by the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Rugby" title="Ulster Rugby">Ulster branch</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Rugby_Football_Union" title="Irish Rugby Football Union">Irish Rugby Football Union</a>, the governing body of <a href="/wiki/Rugby_union" title="Rugby union">rugby union</a> in Ireland.  Ulster is one of the four professional provincial teams in Ireland and competes in the <a href="/wiki/United_Rugby_Championship" title="United Rugby Championship">United Rugby Championship</a> and <a href="/wiki/Heineken_Cup" class="mw-redirect" title="Heineken Cup">European Cup</a>. It won the European Cup in 1999.
</p><p>In international competitions, the <a href="/wiki/Ireland_national_rugby_union_team" title="Ireland national rugby union team">Ireland national rugby union team</a>'s recent successes include four <a href="/wiki/Triple_Crown_(rugby_union)" title="Triple Crown (rugby union)">Triple Crowns</a> between 2004 and 2009 and a <a href="/wiki/Grand_Slam_(rugby_union)" title="Grand Slam (rugby union)">Grand Slam</a> in 2009 in the <a href="/wiki/Six_Nations_Championship" title="Six Nations Championship">Six Nations Championship</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Cricket">Cricket</span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Ireland_cricket_team" title="Ireland cricket team">Ireland cricket team</a> represents both Northern Ireland and the Republic of Ireland. It is a full member of the <a href="/wiki/International_Cricket_Council" title="International Cricket Council">International Cricket Council</a>, having been granted <a href="/wiki/Test_cricket" title="Test cricket">Test status</a> and full membership by the ICC in June 2017. The side competes in Test cricket, the highest level of competitive cricket in the international arena, and is one of the 12 full-member countries of the ICC.
</p><p>
Ireland men's side has played in the <a href="/wiki/Ireland_at_the_Cricket_World_Cup" title="Ireland at the Cricket World Cup">Cricket World Cup</a> and <a href="/wiki/ICC_Men%27s_T20_World_Cup" title="ICC Men&#39;s T20 World Cup">T20 World Cup</a> and has won the <a href="/wiki/ICC_Intercontinental_Cup" title="ICC Intercontinental Cup">ICC Intercontinental Cup</a> four times. The <a href="/wiki/Ireland_women%27s_cricket_team" title="Ireland women&#39;s cricket team">women's side</a> has played in the <a href="/wiki/Women%27s_Cricket_World_Cup" title="Women&#39;s Cricket World Cup">Women's World Cup</a>. One of the men's side's regular international venues is <a href="/wiki/Stormont_(cricket_ground)" title="Stormont (cricket ground)">Stormont</a> in Belfast.</p><div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg/150px-Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="248" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg/225px-Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg/300px-Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg 2x" data-file-width="302" data-file-height="500" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Peter_Canavan_-_SFC_2005_cc_3.0.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Peter_Canavan" title="Peter Canavan">Peter Canavan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tyrone_GAA" title="Tyrone GAA">Tyrone</a> captain 2003</div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Gaelic_games">Gaelic games</span></h4>
<p>Gaelic games include <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_football" title="Gaelic football">Gaelic football</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hurling" title="Hurling">hurling</a> (and <a href="/wiki/Camogie" title="Camogie">camogie</a>), <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_handball" title="Gaelic handball">Gaelic handball</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rounders" title="Rounders">rounders</a>. Of the four, football is the most popular in Northern Ireland.  Players play for local clubs with the best being selected for their county teams. The <a href="/wiki/Ulster_GAA" title="Ulster GAA">Ulster GAA</a> is the branch of the <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_Athletic_Association" title="Gaelic Athletic Association">Gaelic Athletic Association</a> that is responsible for the nine counties of Ulster, which include the six of Northern Ireland.
</p><p>These nine county teams participate in the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Senior_Football_Championship" title="Ulster Senior Football Championship">Ulster Senior Football Championship</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Senior_Hurling_Championship" title="Ulster Senior Hurling Championship">Ulster Senior Hurling Championship</a>, <a href="/wiki/All-Ireland_Senior_Football_Championship" title="All-Ireland Senior Football Championship">All-Ireland Senior Football Championship</a> and <a href="/wiki/All-Ireland_Senior_Hurling_Championship" title="All-Ireland Senior Hurling Championship">All-Ireland Senior Hurling Championship</a>.
</p><p>Recent successes for Northern Ireland teams include <a href="/wiki/Armagh_GAA" title="Armagh GAA">Armagh</a>'s 2002 <a href="/wiki/All-Ireland_Senior_Football_Championship" title="All-Ireland Senior Football Championship">All-Ireland Senior Football Championship</a> win and <a href="/wiki/Tyrone_GAA" title="Tyrone GAA">Tyrone GAA</a>'s wins in 2003, 2005, 2008, and 2021.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Golf">Golf</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_(crowd,_landscape_orientation).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_%28crowd%2C_landscape_orientation%29.jpg/220px-Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_%28crowd%2C_landscape_orientation%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="132" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_%28crowd%2C_landscape_orientation%29.jpg/330px-Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_%28crowd%2C_landscape_orientation%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_%28crowd%2C_landscape_orientation%29.jpg/440px-Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_%28crowd%2C_landscape_orientation%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="540" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Rory_McIlroy_watches_drive_flight_(crowd,_landscape_orientation).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Prominent Northern Irish golfer <a href="/wiki/Rory_McIlroy" title="Rory McIlroy">Rory McIlroy</a></div></div></div>
<p>Perhaps Northern Ireland's most notable successes in professional sport have come in golf. Northern Ireland had three major champions in the space of just 14 months from the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Open_(golf)" title="U.S. Open (golf)">U.S. Open</a> in <a href="/wiki/2010_U.S._Open_(golf)" title="2010 U.S. Open (golf)">2010</a> to <a href="/wiki/The_Open_Championship" title="The Open Championship">The Open Championship</a> in <a href="/wiki/2011_Open_Championship" title="2011 Open Championship">2011</a>. Notable golfers include <a href="/wiki/Fred_Daly_(golfer)" title="Fred Daly (golfer)">Fred Daly</a> (winner of The Open in <a href="/wiki/1947_Open_Championship" title="1947 Open Championship">1947</a>), <a href="/wiki/Ryder_Cup" title="Ryder Cup">Ryder Cup</a> players <a href="/wiki/Ronan_Rafferty" title="Ronan Rafferty">Ronan Rafferty</a> and <a href="/wiki/David_Feherty" title="David Feherty">David Feherty</a>, leading <a href="/wiki/European_Tour" class="mw-redirect" title="European Tour">European Tour</a> professionals <a href="/wiki/David_Jones_(golfer)" title="David Jones (golfer)">David Jones</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michael_Hoey_(golfer)" title="Michael Hoey (golfer)">Michael Hoey</a> (a five-time winner on the tour) and <a href="/wiki/Gareth_Maybin" title="Gareth Maybin">Gareth Maybin</a>, as well as three recent major winners <a href="/wiki/Graeme_McDowell" title="Graeme McDowell">Graeme McDowell</a> (winner of the U.S. Open in 2010, the first European to do so since 1970), <a href="/wiki/Rory_McIlroy" title="Rory McIlroy">Rory McIlroy</a> (winner of four <a href="/wiki/Men%27s_major_golf_championships" title="Men&#39;s major golf championships">majors</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Darren_Clarke" title="Darren Clarke">Darren Clarke</a> (winner of The Open in 2011).<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> Northern Ireland has also contributed several players to the Great Britain and Ireland <a href="/wiki/Walker_Cup" title="Walker Cup">Walker Cup</a> team, including <a href="/wiki/Alan_Dunbar" title="Alan Dunbar">Alan Dunbar</a> and Paul Cutler who played on the victorious 2011 team in Scotland. Dunbar also won <a href="/wiki/The_Amateur_Championship" title="The Amateur Championship">The Amateur Championship</a> in 2012, at Royal Troon.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Golfing_Union_of_Ireland" title="Golfing Union of Ireland">Golfing Union of Ireland</a>, the governing body for men's and boy's amateur golf throughout Ireland and the oldest golfing union in the world, was founded in Belfast in 1891. Northern Ireland's golf courses include the Royal Belfast Golf Club (the earliest, formed in 1881), <a href="/wiki/Royal_Portrush_Golf_Club" title="Royal Portrush Golf Club">Royal Portrush Golf Club</a>, which is the only course outside Great Britain to have hosted <a href="/wiki/The_Open_Championship" title="The Open Championship">The Open Championship</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Royal_County_Down_Golf_Club" title="Royal County Down Golf Club">Royal County Down Golf Club</a> (<i><a href="/wiki/Golf_Digest" title="Golf Digest">Golf Digest</a></i> magazine's top-rated course outside the United States).<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Snooker">Snooker</span></h3>
<p>Northern Ireland has produced two world <a href="/wiki/Snooker" title="Snooker">snooker</a> champions; <a href="/wiki/Alex_Higgins" title="Alex Higgins">Alex Higgins</a>, who won the title in 1972 and 1982, and <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Taylor" title="Dennis Taylor">Dennis Taylor</a>, who won in 1985. The highest-ranked Northern Ireland professional on the world circuit presently is <a href="/wiki/Mark_Allen_(snooker_player)" title="Mark Allen (snooker player)">Mark Allen</a> from <a href="/wiki/County_Antrim" title="County Antrim">County Antrim</a>. The sport is governed locally by the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Billiards_and_Snooker_Association" title="Northern Ireland Billiards and Snooker Association">Northern Ireland Billiards and Snooker Association</a> who run regular ranking tournaments and competitions.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Motorsport">Motorsport</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Motorcycle_racing">Motorcycle racing</span></h4>
<p><a href="/wiki/Motorcycle_racing" title="Motorcycle racing">Motorcycle racing</a> is a particularly popular sport during the summer months, with the main meetings of the season attracting some of the largest crowds to any outdoor sporting event in the whole of <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Two of the three major international road race meetings are held in Northern Ireland, these being the <a href="/wiki/North_West_200" title="North West 200">North West 200</a><sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Ulster_Grand_Prix" title="Ulster Grand Prix">Ulster Grand Prix</a>. In addition racing on purpose built circuits take place at <a href="/wiki/Kirkistown_Circuit" title="Kirkistown Circuit">Kirkistown</a> and Bishop's Court,<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> whilst smaller road race meetings are held such as the <a href="/wiki/Cookstown" title="Cookstown">Cookstown 100</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Armoy,_County_Antrim" class="mw-redirect" title="Armoy, County Antrim">Armoy</a> Road Races<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Tandragee" title="Tandragee">Tandragee</a> 100<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> all of which form part of the Irish National Road Race Championships<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> and which have produced some of the greatest motorcycle racers in the history of the sport, notably <a href="/wiki/Joey_Dunlop" title="Joey Dunlop">Joey Dunlop</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Motor_racing">Motor racing</span></h4>
<p>Although Northern Ireland lacks an international automobile racecourse, two Northern Irish drivers have finished inside the top two of <a href="/wiki/Formula_One" title="Formula One">Formula One</a>, with <a href="/wiki/John_Watson_(racing_driver)" title="John Watson (racing driver)">John Watson</a> achieving the feat in the <a href="/wiki/1982_Formula_One_season" class="mw-redirect" title="1982 Formula One season">1982 Formula One season</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eddie_Irvine" title="Eddie Irvine">Eddie Irvine</a> doing the same in <a href="/wiki/1999_Formula_One_season" class="mw-redirect" title="1999 Formula One season">1999 Formula One season</a>. The largest course and the only <a href="/wiki/Motor_Sports_Association" class="mw-redirect" title="Motor Sports Association">Motor Sports Association</a>-licensed track for UK-wide competition is <a href="/wiki/Kirkistown_Circuit" title="Kirkistown Circuit">Kirkistown Circuit</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rugby_league">Rugby league</span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Ireland_national_rugby_league_team" title="Ireland national rugby league team">Ireland national rugby league team</a> has participated in the <a href="/wiki/Rugby_League_Emerging_Nations_Tournament" class="mw-redirect" title="Rugby League Emerging Nations Tournament">Emerging Nations Tournament</a> (1995), the Super League World Nines (1996), the World Cup (2000, 2008, 2013, 2017, 2021), European Nations Cup (since 2003) and Victory Cup (2004).
</p><p>The Ireland A rugby league team competes annually in the Amateur Four Nations competition (since 2002) and the St Patrick's Day Challenge (since 1995).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ice_hockey">Ice hockey</span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Belfast_Giants" title="Belfast Giants">Belfast Giants</a> have competed in the <a href="/wiki/Elite_Ice_Hockey_League" title="Elite Ice Hockey League">Elite Ice Hockey League</a> since the 2000–01 season and are the sole Northern Irish team in the league. The team's roster has featured Northern Irish-born players such as <a href="/wiki/Mark_Morrison_(ice_hockey,_born_1982)" title="Mark Morrison (ice hockey, born 1982)">Mark Morrison</a>, <a href="/wiki/Graeme_Walton" title="Graeme Walton">Graeme Walton</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Gareth_Roberts_(ice_hockey)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Gareth Roberts (ice hockey) (page does not exist)">Gareth Roberts</a> among others.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Geraldine_Heaney" title="Geraldine Heaney">Geraldine Heaney</a>, an Olympic gold medalist and one of the first women inducted into the <a href="/wiki/IIHF_Hall_of_Fame" title="IIHF Hall of Fame">IIHF Hall of Fame</a>, competed internationally for <a href="/wiki/Canada_women%27s_national_ice_hockey_team" title="Canada women&#39;s national ice hockey team">Canada</a> but was born in Northern Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Owen_Nolan" title="Owen Nolan">Owen Nolan</a>, (born 12 February 1972) is a Canadian former professional ice hockey player born in Northern Ireland. He was drafted 1st overall in the <a href="/wiki/1990_NHL_Draft" class="mw-redirect" title="1990 NHL Draft">1990 NHL Draft</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Nordiques" title="Quebec Nordiques">Quebec Nordiques</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Professional_wrestling">Professional wrestling</span></h3>
<p>In 2007, after the closure of UCW (Ulster Championship Wrestling) which was a wrestling promotion, PWU formed, standing for Pro Wrestling Ulster. The wrestling promotion features championships, former <a href="/wiki/List_of_WWE_personnel" title="List of WWE personnel">WWE superstars</a> and local independent wrestlers. Events and IPPV's throughout Northern Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Northern_Ireland" title="Education in Northern Ireland">Education in Northern Ireland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg/220px-Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg/330px-Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg/440px-Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Queen%27s_University_Belfast_2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_University_Belfast" title="Queen&#39;s University Belfast">Queen's University Belfast</a></div></div></div>
<p>Unlike most areas of the United Kingdom, in the last year of primary school, many children sit entrance examinations for <a href="/wiki/Grammar_schools_in_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Grammar schools in the United Kingdom">grammar schools</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Integrated_Education" class="mw-redirect" title="Integrated Education">Integrated schools</a>, which attempt to ensure a balance in enrolment between pupils of Protestant, Roman Catholic, and other faiths (or none), are becoming increasingly popular, although Northern Ireland still has a primarily <i>de facto</i> religiously segregated education system. In the primary school sector, 40 schools (8.9% of the total number) are integrated schools and 32 (7.2% of the total number) are <a href="/wiki/Gaelscoileanna" class="mw-redirect" title="Gaelscoileanna">Gaelscoileanna</a> (Irish language-medium schools).
</p><p>The main universities in Northern Ireland are <a href="/wiki/Queen%27s_University_Belfast" title="Queen&#39;s University Belfast">Queen's University Belfast</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ulster_University" title="Ulster University">Ulster University</a>, and the distance learning <a href="/wiki/Open_University" title="Open University">Open University</a> which has a regional office in Belfast.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Wildlife">Wildlife</span></h2>
<p>356 species of marine <a href="/wiki/Algae" title="Algae">algae</a> have been recorded in the northeast of Ireland. As Counties Londonderry, Antrim, and Down are the only three counties of Northern Ireland with a shoreline this will apply to all of Northern Ireland. 77 species are considered rare having been recorded rarely.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Media_and_communications">Media and communications</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Media_in_Northern_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Media in Northern Ireland">Media in Northern Ireland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Media_in_the_Republic_of_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Media in the Republic of Ireland">Media in the Republic of Ireland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg/220px-BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg/330px-BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg/440px-BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:BBC_Northern_Ireland_Belfast.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Broadcasting_House,_Belfast" title="Broadcasting House, Belfast">Broadcasting House, Belfast</a>, home of <a href="/wiki/BBC_Northern_Ireland" title="BBC Northern Ireland">BBC Northern Ireland</a></div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a> has a division called <a href="/wiki/BBC_Northern_Ireland" title="BBC Northern Ireland">BBC Northern Ireland</a> with headquarters in Belfast and operates <a href="/wiki/BBC_One_Northern_Ireland" title="BBC One Northern Ireland">BBC One Northern Ireland</a> and <a href="/wiki/BBC_Two_Northern_Ireland" title="BBC Two Northern Ireland">BBC Two Northern Ireland</a>. As well as broadcasting standard UK-wide programmes, BBC NI produces local content, including a news break-out called <a href="/wiki/BBC_Newsline" title="BBC Newsline">BBC Newsline</a>. The <a href="/wiki/ITV_(TV_network)" title="ITV (TV network)">ITV</a> franchise in Northern Ireland is <a href="/wiki/UTV_(TV_channel)" title="UTV (TV channel)">UTV</a>. The state-owned <a href="/wiki/Channel_4" title="Channel 4">Channel 4</a> and the privately owned <a href="/wiki/Channel_5_(British_TV_channel)" title="Channel 5 (British TV channel)">Channel 5</a> also broadcast in Northern Ireland. Access is also available to satellite and cable services.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> All Northern Ireland viewers must obtain a UK <a href="/wiki/Television_licensing_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Television licensing in the United Kingdom">TV licence</a> to watch live television transmissions or use <a href="/wiki/BBC_iPlayer" title="BBC iPlayer">BBC iPlayer</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/RT%C3%89" title="RTÉ">RTÉ</a>, the national broadcaster of the Republic of Ireland, is available over the air to most parts of Northern Ireland via reception overspill of the Republic's <a href="/wiki/Saorview" title="Saorview">Saorview</a> service,<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> or via satellite and cable. Since the digital TV switchover, <a href="/wiki/RT%C3%89_One" title="RTÉ One">RTÉ One</a>, <a href="/wiki/RT%C3%892" title="RTÉ2">RTÉ2</a> and the Irish-language channel <a href="/wiki/TG4" title="TG4">TG4</a>, are now available over the air on the UK's <a href="/wiki/Freeview_(UK)" title="Freeview (UK)">Freeview</a> system from transmitters within Northern Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> Although they are transmitted in standard definition, a Freeview HD box or television is required for reception.
</p><p>As well as the standard UK-wide radio stations from the BBC, Northern Ireland is home to many local radio stations, such as <a href="/wiki/Cool_FM" title="Cool FM">Cool FM</a>, <a href="/wiki/Q_Radio_Network" title="Q Radio Network">Q Radio</a>, <a href="/wiki/Downtown_Radio" title="Downtown Radio">Downtown Radio</a> and <a href="/wiki/U105" title="U105">U105</a>. The BBC has two regional radio stations which broadcast in Northern Ireland, <a href="/wiki/BBC_Radio_Ulster" title="BBC Radio Ulster">BBC Radio Ulster</a> and <a href="/wiki/BBC_Radio_Foyle" title="BBC Radio Foyle">BBC Radio Foyle</a>.
</p><p>Besides the UK and Irish national newspapers, there are three main regional newspapers published in Northern Ireland. These are the <i><a href="/wiki/Belfast_Telegraph" title="Belfast Telegraph">Belfast Telegraph</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Irish_News" title="The Irish News">The Irish News</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/The_News_Letter" class="mw-redirect" title="The News Letter">The News Letter</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Audit_Bureau_of_Circulations_(UK)" title="Audit Bureau of Circulations (UK)">Audit Bureau of Circulations (UK)</a> the average daily circulation for these three titles in 2018 was:
</p>
<dl><dd><table class="sortable wikitable">

<p>Northern Ireland uses the same telecommunications and postal services as the rest of the United Kingdom at standard domestic rates and there are no mobile roaming charges between Great Britain and Northern Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> People in Northern Ireland who live close to the border with the Republic of Ireland may inadvertently switch over to the Irish mobile networks, causing international roaming fees to be applied.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> Calls from landlines in Northern Ireland to numbers in the Republic of Ireland are charged at the same rate as those to numbers in Great Britain, while landline numbers in Northern Ireland can similarly be called from the Republic of Ireland at domestic rates, using the <a href="/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_the_Republic_of_Ireland#Calls_to_Northern_Ireland" title="Telephone numbers in the Republic of Ireland">048</a> prefix.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-alphabeticalNI-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-alphabeticalNI_12-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-alphabeticalNI_12-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-alphabeticalNI_12-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-alphabeticalNI_12-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFDunnH._Dawson2000" class="citation cs2">Dunn, S.; H. Dawson (2000), <i>An Alphabetical Listing of Word, Name and Place in Northern Ireland and the Living Language of Conflict</i>, <a href="/wiki/Lewiston,_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Lewiston, New York">Lewiston, New York</a>: <a href="/wiki/Edwin_Mellen_Press" title="Edwin Mellen Press">Edwin Mellen Press</a>, <q>One specific problem – in both general and particular senses – is to know what to call Northern Ireland itself: in the general sense, it is not a country, or a province, or a state – although some refer to it contemptuously as a statelet: the least controversial word appears to be jurisdiction, but this might change.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=An+Alphabetical+Listing+of+Word%2C+Name+and+Place+in+Northern+Ireland+and+the+Living+Language+of+Conflict&amp;rft.place=Lewiston%2C+New+York&amp;rft.pub=Edwin+Mellen+Press&amp;rft.date=2000&amp;rft.aulast=Dunn&amp;rft.aufirst=S.&amp;rft.au=H.+Dawson&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANorthern+Ireland" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-interpretingNI-13"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-interpretingNI_13-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-interpretingNI_13-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-interpretingNI_13-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-interpretingNI_13-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-interpretingNI_13-4"><sup><i><b>e</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-interpretingNI_13-5"><sup><i><b>f</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFWhyteG._FitzGerald1991" class="citation cs2">Whyte, J.; G. FitzGerald (1991), <i>Interpreting Northern Ireland</i>, Oxford: Oxford University Press, <q>One problem must be adverted to in writing about Northern Ireland. This is the question of what name to give to the various geographical entities. These names can be controversial, with the choice often revealing one's political preferences. ... some refer to Northern Ireland as a 'province'. That usage can arouse irritation particularly among nationalists, who claim the title 'province' should be properly reserved to the four historic provinces of Ireland-Ulster, Leinster, Munster, and Connacht. If I want to a label to apply to Northern Ireland I shall call it a 'region'. Unionists should find that title as acceptable as 'province': Northern Ireland appears as a region in the regional statistics of the United Kingdom published by the British government.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Interpreting+Northern+Ireland&amp;rft.place=Oxford&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press&amp;rft.date=1991&amp;rft.aulast=Whyte&amp;rft.aufirst=J.&amp;rft.au=G.+FitzGerald&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANorthern+Ireland" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-placeApart-14"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-placeApart_14-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-placeApart_14-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-placeApart_14-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-placeApart_14-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-placeApart_14-4"><sup><i><b>e</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-placeApart_14-5"><sup><i><b>f</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFMurphy1979" class="citation cs2">Murphy, D. (1979), <i><a href="/wiki/A_Place_Apart" title="A Place Apart">A Place Apart</a></i>, London: Penguin Books, <q>Next – what noun is appropriate to Northern Ireland? 'Province' won't do since one-third of the province is on the wrong side of the border. 'State' implies more self-determination than Northern Ireland has ever had and 'country' or 'nation' are blatantly absurd. 'Colony' has overtones that would be resented by both communities and 'statelet' sounds too patronizing, though outsiders might consider it more precise than anything else; so one is left with the unsatisfactory word 'region'.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=A+Place+Apart&amp;rft.place=London&amp;rft.pub=Penguin+Books&amp;rft.date=1979&amp;rft.aulast=Murphy&amp;rft.aufirst=D.&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANorthern+Ireland" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

Retrieved <span class="nowrap">22 September</span> 2022</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=BBC+News&amp;rft.atitle=Census%3A+Northern+Ireland+population+just+under+2m&amp;rft.date=2022-05-24&amp;rft.aulast=Simpson&amp;rft.aufirst=Mark&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.bbc.co.uk%2Fnews%2Fuk-northern-ireland-61562493&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANorthern+Ireland" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-McKittrick-McVea_p5-18"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-McKittrick-McVea_p5_18-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-McKittrick-McVea_p5_18-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">David McKittrick &amp; David McVea. <i>Making Sense of the Troubles</i>. Briefing No. 16, January 2006; Retrieved from <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20060822152404/http://www.institute-of-governance.org/forum/Leverhulme/briefing_pdfs/IoG_Briefing_16.pdf">"IoG_Briefing"</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. <li id="cite_note-NI_logo-193"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-NI_logo_193-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFNorthern_Ireland_Assembly_Information_Office" class="citation web cs1">Northern Ireland Assembly Information Office. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20101013063735/http://www.niassembly.gov.uk/io/logo.htm">"Northern Ireland Assembly logo"</a>. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">13 November</span> 2010</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=Northern+Ireland+Assembly+logo&amp;rft.pub=Niassembly.gov.uk&amp;rft.au=Northern+Ireland+Assembly+Information+Office&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.niassembly.gov.uk%2Fio%2Flogo.htm&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANorthern+Ireland" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-194"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-194">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFAlan_O&#39;Day1987" class="citation cs2">Alan O'Day, ed. (1987), <i>Reactions To Irish Nationalism, 1865–1914</i>, London: Hambledon Press, <q>With the Rising of 1916 a break took place in national symbolism which was most visibly manifested in the national flag and the anthem which the young Irish nation accepted. The demise of the Parliamentary Party stands in direct parallel to the just as rapidly diminishing power of its symbols. The green flag and 'God save Ireland' began to be discredited as symbols of constitutional nationalism and, instead, the symbols of revolutionary nationalism gained popularity as the majority of the Irish people identified themselves with the political aims of the Easter revolutionaries. The use of symbols made apparent that the occurrences of 1916 initiated a new epoch in Irish history much in the same as the Union of 1801 and the Famine of 1845–8 did.<p>Both the national flag and the national anthem of present-day Ireland drive origins directly from the Rising. At first, it still appeared as if the revolutionaries would take over the old symbols because on the roof of their headquarters, the Dublin General Post Office, a green flag with the harp was hoisted next to the republican tricolour although with the inscription 'Irish Republic'. Even 'Got save Ireland' was sung by the revolutionaries during Easter week. But after the failure of the Rising and the subsequent executions of the leading revolutionaries the tricolour and 'The Soldier's Song' became more and more popular as symbols of the rebellion.</p></q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Reactions+To+Irish+Nationalism%2C+1865%E2%80%931914&amp;rft.place=London&amp;rft.pub=Hambledon+Press&amp;rft.date=1987&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ANorthern+Ireland" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
